========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess what brings me in is... I just can't shake this heavy feeling that I've completely ruined things for everyone. I've been falling so far behind at work lately. I do computer support, mostly helping people with their network issues and software glitches. You wouldn't believe how many people don't know how to just restart a router. It's usually just a little button on the back, right next to the power cord, or you just unplug it for ten seconds, but they call in completely panicking about their Wi-Fi. I used to be able to clear those help desk tickets so fast... but yeah, I fell behind on all of it. 

And because of that, I ended up losing control of the apartment situation. My landlord is this guy who always wears these really loud Hawaiian shirts, even in the dead of winter, which I never really understood. Like, doesn't he get freezing cold? He drives this old beat-up Buick that always smells like cheap cigars... but anyway, I just feel like I've messed it all up. Like I deserve whatever bad thing happens to me next.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I guess so. Most days I wake up with this... this flat, heavy sadness. It's like the things that used to reset me just don’t reach me anymore. I used to love putting on a record, you know? I have this huge collection of vinyl. Mostly 70s and 80s rock. I spent years hunting down original pressings at these little hole-in-the-wall record stores. There was this one shop downtown, run by this guy who looked exactly like Jerry Garcia, and he always smelled like patchouli and old paper. He had the best stuff hidden in the back crates. But now... I don't even turn the turntable on. 

I used to cook a lot, too, and tinker with electronics. I built my own stereo receiver from scratch a few years ago. Sourced all the capacitors and resistors myself. Soldering is actually really relaxing, or it used to be. You have to get the iron to just the right temperature, usually around 600 degrees, and use a good rosin-core solder so the joints are clean. If you use cheap solder, it gets all cloudy and brittle, and then you have to desolder the whole thing with a copper wick and start over. But yeah, none of that feels good anymore. It just feels like... nothing.

It's usually just a little button on the back, right next to the power cord, or you just unplug it for ten seconds, but they call in completely panicking about their Wi-Fi. My landlord is this guy who always wears these really loud Hawaiian shirts, even in the dead of winter, which I never really understood. Like, doesn't he get freezing cold? Yeah, exactly like I was saying earlier. I just... I really can't shake this feeling that I've ruined things for everyone. I fell so far behind at work, and then letting the apartment situation get completely out of control... I just feel like a total failure. Like I deserve whatever bad thing happens to me next. 

The apartment building is actually this really old brick walk-up, built in like 1924, I think. It has those ancient cast-iron radiators that clank so loud in the middle of the night. It sounds like someone is taking a metal wrench to the pipes, just banging on them at 3 AM. The super said it's because the boiler pressure is too high, which causes the steam to hit trapped condensation in the pipes. They call it a water hammer. You're actually supposed to put little wooden shims under the feet of the radiator to tilt it, just slightly, so the water drains back down to the boiler, but nobody ever does. I meant to fix mine, actually. I bought the shims from the hardware store and everything. But... yeah. I didn't do that either. I just feel like I mess everything up.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I've noticed that. It's really obvious at work. I'll just be sitting there, and my hands just hover over the keyboard. It takes me so much longer to answer even the simplest support tickets. It's almost like my brain and body are running on this delayed connection. 

Speaking of connections, we had this massive server migration at work a few months ago, right before I started feeling really bad. We were switching from these old legacy physical servers to a fully cloud-based infrastructure. It was supposed to be seamless, but nothing ever is in IT. We had to map all these virtual LANs, and the IP routing tables were a complete mess because the previous guy who set them up didn't document anything. He used this really obscure naming convention for the subnets based on Lord of the Rings characters. Like, the main database was 'Rivendell' and the backup server was 'Helm's Deep.' It was funny for about five minutes until you actually had to troubleshoot a packet loss issue at 2 AM. I spent three whole weekends just untangling the DNS records. But yeah... everything just feels delayed now. Like I'm moving through molasses.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I guess... I mean, I'm not really sure if I'm more tired than usual. I don't think so. I haven't really noticed a huge difference in my energy, to be honest. It's just hard to tell sometimes, you know? 

Like, when I was in college, I used to pull these crazy all-nighters all the time. I'd stay up for 36 hours straight working on a single coding project. I remember this one time we had to build a basic operating system kernel from scratch for this advanced systems class. We were using C, mostly, and a little bit of Assembly language for the bootloader. Assembly is brutal because you're basically talking directly to the CPU registers. You have to manage the memory addresses manually, and if you mess up a single pointer, the whole system just crashes and throws a segmentation fault. We lived on those terrible energy drinks that tasted like battery acid and cheap pizza from this place down the street that stayed open until 4 AM. The guy who worked there, Tony, he always gave us extra garlic knots because he felt bad for us. We'd stumble out of the computer lab at dawn, completely exhausted, but it was this good kind of tired. A productive tired. Now, I just... I don't know. I guess I haven't really paid attention to my energy levels lately. I just kind of exist.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I haven't really noticed any big changes, to be honest. I mean, I guess I'm eating about the same as I usually do. I haven't been weighing myself or anything, so I couldn't tell you if I've lost weight. 

My scale actually broke a few months ago. It was one of those smart scales that syncs via Bluetooth to an app on your phone, so it tracks your body fat percentage and bone mass and all that. But the Bluetooth module on it just completely failed. I tried resetting it, unpairing it, clearing the Bluetooth cache on my phone... nothing. It's weird how dependent we get on those little microcontrollers. Like, I used to just step on a regular analog scale with the little dial that spins, but now if my scale can't send a data packet to a server halfway across the country, I feel like the weigh-in didn't even happen. I actually took the back off the scale to see if maybe a solder joint had come loose on the PCB, but it's all surface-mount components, so unless you have a hot air rework station and a microscope, you're pretty much out of luck. Anyway, no, I don't think my appetite has changed much.

If you use cheap solder, it gets all cloudy and brittle, and then you have to desolder the whole thing with a copper wick and start over. But yeah, none of that feels good anymore. It just feels like... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel that you're a failure or that you've let people down, even if they haven't directly said so?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly like I was saying earlier. I just... I really can't shake this feeling that I've ruined things for everyone. I fell so far behind at work, and then letting the apartment situation get completely out of control... I just feel like a total failure. Like I deserve whatever bad thing happens to me next. \n\nThe apartment building is actually this really old brick walk-up, built in like 1924, I think. It has those ancient cast-iron radiators that clank so loud in the middle of the night. It sounds like someone is taking a metal wrench to the pipes, just banging on them at 3 AM. The super said it's because the boiler pressure is too high, which causes the steam to hit trapped condensation in the pipes. They call it a water hammer. You're actually supposed to put little wooden shims under the feet of the radiator to tilt it, just slightly, so the water drains back down to the boiler, but nobody ever does. I meant to fix mine, actually. I bought the shims from the hardware store and everything. But... yeah. I didn't do that either. I just feel like I mess everything up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been moving more slowly than usual, or have trouble sitting still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I've noticed that. It's really obvious at work. I'll just be sitting there, and my hands just hover over the keyboard. It takes me so much longer to answer even the simplest support tickets. It's almost like my brain and body are running on this delayed connection. \n\nSpeaking of connections, we had this massive server migration at work a few months ago, right before I started feeling really bad. We were switching from these old legacy physical servers to a fully cloud-based infrastructure. It was supposed to be seamless, but nothing ever is in IT. We had to map all these virtual LANs, and the IP routing tables were a complete mess because the previous guy who set them up didn't document anything. He used this really obscure naming convention for the subnets based on Lord of the Rings characters. Like, the main database was 'Rivendell' and the backup server was 'Helm's Deep.' It was funny for about five minutes until you actually had to troubleshoot a packet loss issue at 2 AM. I spent three whole weekends just untangling the DNS records. But yeah... everything just feels delayed now. Like I'm moving through molasses.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or having little energy most days, even when you haven't been doing much?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess... I mean, I'm not really sure if I'm more tired than usual. I don't think so. I haven't really noticed a huge difference in my energy, to be honest. It's just hard to tell sometimes, you know? \n\nLike, when I was in college, I used to pull these crazy all-nighters all the time. I'd stay up for 36 hours straight working on a single coding project. I remember this one time we had to build a basic operating system kernel from scratch for this advanced systems class. We were using C, mostly, and a little bit of Assembly language for the bootloader. Assembly is brutal because you're basically talking directly to the CPU registers. You have to manage the memory addresses manually, and if you mess up a single pointer, the whole system just crashes and throws a segmentation fault. We lived on those terrible energy drinks that tasted like battery acid and cheap pizza from this place down the street that stayed open until 4 AM. The guy who worked there, Tony, he always gave us extra garlic knots because he felt bad for us. We'd stumble out of the computer lab at dawn, completely exhausted, but it was this good kind of tired. A productive tired. Now, I just... I don't know. I guess I haven't really paid attention to my energy levels lately. I just kind of exist.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating much less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any big changes, to be honest. I mean, I guess I'm eating about the same as I usually do. I haven't been weighing myself or anything, so I couldn't tell you if I've lost weight. \n\nMy scale actually broke a few months ago. It was one of those smart scales that syncs via Bluetooth to an app on your phone, so it tracks your body fat percentage and bone mass and all that. But the Bluetooth module on it just completely failed. I tried resetting it, unpairing it, clearing the Bluetooth cache on my phone... nothing. It's weird how dependent we get on those little microcontrollers. Like, I used to just step on a regular analog scale with the little dial that spins, but now if my scale can't send a data packet to a server halfway across the country, I feel like the weigh-in didn't even happen. I actually took the back off the scale to see if maybe a solder joint had come loose on the PCB, but it's all surface-mount components, so unless you have a hot air rework station and a microscope, you're pretty much out of luck. Anyway, no, I don't think my appetite has changed much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I don't have a plan to hurt myself or anything like that. But... I do catch myself thinking sometimes that it would just be easier if I didn't wake up, you know? Like, if I just faded out and nobody had to deal with me anymore. It's just this passing thought that creeps in.

Speaking of waking up, I've been having the weirdest issue with my alarm clock lately. Well, it's not a clock, it's my phone, obviously. But I set up this whole smart home routine using Home Assistant—it's this open-source home automation platform that runs on a Raspberry Pi I have tucked behind my router. Anyway, I programmed it so that when my phone alarm goes off, it triggers a webhook that slowly fades up the smart bulbs in my bedroom over 15 minutes, to simulate a sunrise. It's supposed to be better for your circadian rhythm than just waking up in a dark room to a blaring noise. But last week, the API integration between Home Assistant and the smart bulb bridge just broke. No error logs, no timeout warnings, nothing. Just completely stopped talking to each other. I spent hours looking at the JSON payloads trying to figure out if the authentication token expired or if the manufacturer changed their API endpoints without telling anyone. It's so frustrating when these companies have closed ecosystems and you have to reverse-engineer their protocols just to turn on a light bulb. But yeah... sometimes I just think it would be easier not to wake up at all.

Anyway, I programmed it so that when my phone alarm goes off, it triggers a webhook that slowly fades up the smart bulbs in my bedroom over 15 minutes, to simulate a sunrise. It's supposed to be better for your circadian rhythm than just waking up in a dark room to a blaring noise. But last week, the API integration between Home Assistant and the smart bulb bridge just broke. No error logs, no timeout warnings, nothing. Just completely stopped talking to each other. It's so frustrating when these companies have closed ecosystems and you have to reverse-engineer their protocols just to turn on a light bulb. Yeah, I guess I have noticed that a bit. At work, I'll be looking at a simple support ticket, and my hands will just hover over the keyboard. It's like it takes me way longer to type out a basic response, almost like my brain and my body are running on a delayed connection, you know? Like high latency.

We use this screen-sharing software at work, and sometimes the connection is so bad that I'll click on a user's start menu and it takes like five seconds for it to actually open on my screen. It's infuriating. It usually happens when they're working from home on some terrible consumer-grade router that their ISP gave them. Those things are garbage. I always tell people to just buy a decent mesh network system or at least a standalone router, but they never listen. They just complain that their internet is "slow," when really it's just their local network that's congested because their kids are streaming 4K video and playing online games at the same time. Can you tell me what’s been bothering you or what brings you here today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brings me in is... I just can't shake this heavy feeling that I've completely ruined things for everyone. I've been falling so far behind at work lately. I do computer support, mostly helping people with their network issues and software glitches. You wouldn't believe how many people don't know how to just restart a router. It's usually just a little button on the back, right next to the power cord, or you just unplug it for ten seconds, but they call in completely panicking about their Wi-Fi. I used to be able to clear those help desk tickets so fast... but yeah, I fell behind on all of it. \n\nAnd because of that, I ended up losing control of the apartment situation. Like I deserve whatever bad thing happens to me next.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don't enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you've lost interest in activities you once liked?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. Most days I wake up with this... this flat, heavy sadness. It's like the things that used to reset me just don’t reach me anymore. I used to love putting on a record, you know? I have this huge collection of vinyl. Mostly 70s and 80s rock. I spent years hunting down original pressings at these little hole-in-the-wall record stores. There was this one shop downtown, run by this guy who looked exactly like Jerry Garcia, and he always smelled like patchouli and old paper. He had the best stuff hidden in the back crates. But now... I don't even turn the turntable on. \n\nI used to cook a lot, too, and tinker with electronics. I built my own stereo receiver from scratch a few years ago. Sourced all the capacitors and resistors myself. Soldering is actually really relaxing, or it used to be. You have to get the iron to just the right temperature, usually around 600 degrees, and use a good rosin-core solder so the joints are clean. If you use cheap solder, it gets all cloudy and brittle, and then you have to desolder the whole thing with a copper wick and start over. But yeah, none of that feels good anymore. It just feels like... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel that you're a failure or that you've let people down, even if they haven't directly said so?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly like I was saying earlier. I just... I really can't shake this feeling that I've ruined things for everyone. I fell so far behind at work, and then letting the apartment situation get completely out of control... I just feel like a total failure. Like I deserve whatever bad thing happens to me next. \n\nThe apartment building is actually this really old brick walk-up, built in like 1924, I think. It has those ancient cast-iron radiators that clank so loud in the middle of the night. It sounds like someone is taking a metal wrench to the pipes, just banging on them at 3 AM. The super said it's because the boiler pressure is too high, which causes the steam to hit trapped condensation in the pipes. They call it a water hammer. You're actually supposed to put little wooden shims under the feet of the radiator to tilt it, just slightly, so the water drains back down to the boiler, but nobody ever does. I meant to fix mine, actually. I bought the shims from the hardware store and everything. But... yeah. I didn't do that either. I just feel like I mess everything up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been moving more slowly than usual, or have trouble sitting still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I've noticed that. It's really obvious at work. I'll just be sitting there, and my hands just hover over the keyboard. It takes me so much longer to answer even the simplest support tickets. It's almost like my brain and body are running on this delayed connection. \n\nSpeaking of connections, we had this massive server migration at work a few months ago, right before I started feeling really bad. We were switching from these old legacy physical servers to a fully cloud-based infrastructure. It was supposed to be seamless, but nothing ever is in IT. We had to map all these virtual LANs, and the IP routing tables were a complete mess because the previous guy who set them up didn't document anything. He used this really obscure naming convention for the subnets based on Lord of the Rings characters. Like, the main database was 'Rivendell' and the backup server was 'Helm's Deep.' It was funny for about five minutes until you actually had to troubleshoot a packet loss issue at 2 AM. I spent three whole weekends just untangling the DNS records. But yeah... everything just feels delayed now. Like I'm moving through molasses.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or having little energy most days, even when you haven't been doing much?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess... I mean, I'm not really sure if I'm more tired than usual. I don't think so. I haven't really noticed a huge difference in my energy, to be honest. It's just hard to tell sometimes, you know? \n\nLike, when I was in college, I used to pull these crazy all-nighters all the time. I'd stay up for 36 hours straight working on a single coding project. I remember this one time we had to build a basic operating system kernel from scratch for this advanced systems class. We were using C, mostly, and a little bit of Assembly language for the bootloader. Assembly is brutal because you're basically talking directly to the CPU registers. You have to manage the memory addresses manually, and if you mess up a single pointer, the whole system just crashes and throws a segmentation fault. We lived on those terrible energy drinks that tasted like battery acid and cheap pizza from this place down the street that stayed open until 4 AM. The guy who worked there, Tony, he always gave us extra garlic knots because he felt bad for us. We'd stumble out of the computer lab at dawn, completely exhausted, but it was this good kind of tired. A productive tired. Now, I just... I don't know. I guess I haven't really paid attention to my energy levels lately. I just kind of exist.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating much less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any big changes, to be honest. I mean, I guess I'm eating about the same as I usually do. I haven't been weighing myself or anything, so I couldn't tell you if I've lost weight. \n\nMy scale actually broke a few months ago. It was one of those smart scales that syncs via Bluetooth to an app on your phone, so it tracks your body fat percentage and bone mass and all that. But the Bluetooth module on it just completely failed. I tried resetting it, unpairing it, clearing the Bluetooth cache on my phone... nothing. It's weird how dependent we get on those little microcontrollers. Like, I used to just step on a regular analog scale with the little dial that spins, but now if my scale can't send a data packet to a server halfway across the country, I feel like the weigh-in didn't even happen. I actually took the back off the scale to see if maybe a solder joint had come loose on the PCB, but it's all surface-mount components, so unless you have a hot air rework station and a microscope, you're pretty much out of luck. Anyway, no, I don't think my appetite has changed much.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: I don't have a plan to hurt myself or anything like that. But... I do catch myself thinking sometimes that it would just be easier if I didn't wake up, you know? Like, if I just faded out and nobody had to deal with me anymore. It's just this passing thought that creeps in.\n\nSpeaking of waking up, I've been having the weirdest issue with my alarm clock lately. Well, it's not a clock, it's my phone, obviously. But I set up this whole smart home routine using Home Assistant—it's this open-source home automation platform that runs on a Raspberry Pi I have tucked behind my router. Anyway, I programmed it so that when my phone alarm goes off, it triggers a webhook that slowly fades up the smart bulbs in my bedroom over 15 minutes, to simulate a sunrise. It's supposed to be better for your circadian rhythm than just waking up in a dark room to a blaring noise. But last week, the API integration between Home Assistant and the smart bulb bridge just broke. No error logs, no timeout warnings, nothing. Just completely stopped talking to each other. I spent hours looking at the JSON payloads trying to figure out if the authentication token expired or if the manufacturer changed their API endpoints without telling anyone. It's so frustrating when these companies have closed ecosystems and you have to reverse-engineer their protocols just to turn on a light bulb. But yeah... sometimes I just think it would be easier not to wake up at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to concentrate on tasks, or felt like your thinking has slowed down significantly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have noticed that a bit. At work, I'll be looking at a simple support ticket, and my hands will just hover over the keyboard. It's like it takes me way longer to type out a basic response, almost like my brain and my body are running on a delayed connection, you know? Like high latency.\n\nLatency is the worst, especially when you're trying to do remote support. We use this screen-sharing software at work, and sometimes the connection is so bad that I'll click on a user's start menu and it takes like five seconds for it to actually open on my screen. It's infuriating. It usually happens when they're working from home on some terrible consumer-grade router that their ISP gave them. Those things are garbage. They have terrible routing tables and the Wi-Fi antennas are practically useless if you're more than ten feet away or if there's a wall in the way. I always tell people to just buy a decent mesh network system or at least a standalone router, but they never listen. They just complain that their internet is \"slow,\" when really it's just their local network that's congested because their kids are streaming 4K video and playing online games at the same time. You can't fix physics, you know? Bandwidth isn't infinite. But yeah, that delayed connection feeling... it's kind of like that, but in my head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But the Bluetooth module on it just completely failed. I tried resetting it, unpairing it, clearing the Bluetooth cache on my phone... nothing. It's weird how dependent we get on those little microcontrollers. But I set up this whole smart home routine using Home Assistant—it's this open-source home automation platform that runs on a Raspberry Pi I have tucked behind my router. Anyway, I programmed it so that when my phone alarm goes off, it triggers a webhook that slowly fades up the smart bulbs in my bedroom over 15 minutes, to simulate a sunrise. It's supposed to be better for your circadian rhythm than just waking up in a dark room to a blaring noise. But last week, the API integration between Home Assistant and the smart bulb bridge just broke. No error logs, no timeout warnings, nothing. Just completely stopped talking to each other. I spent hours looking at the JSON payloads trying to figure out if the authentication token expired or if the manufacturer changed their API endpoints without telling anyone. It's so frustrating when these companies have closed ecosystems and you have to reverse-engineer their protocols just to turn on a light bulb. But yeah... sometimes I just think it would be easier not to wake up at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to concentrate on tasks, or felt like your thinking has slowed down significantly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have noticed that a bit. They have terrible routing tables and the Wi-Fi antennas are practically useless if you're more than ten feet away or if there's a wall in the way. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't think I've completely lost interest in everything. I mean, I still like doing some things... I still get a little enjoyment out of messing with my PC setup. 

Actually, I just tried to upgrade my graphics card last weekend. I finally managed to get my hands on a decent one at retail price, which is basically a miracle right now with all the scalpers and bots buying up the inventory in milliseconds. But when I went to install it, I realized my power supply didn't have the right PCIe cables. It's a fully modular power supply, but I must have thrown out the box with all the extra cables years ago when I moved. So I had to order these custom sleeved cables online. They actually look really good, they make the cable management inside the case so much cleaner. Cable management is actually super important, not just because it looks nice, but if you have this massive rat's nest of wires blocking the front intake fans, you're going to choke off the airflow to the motherboard VRMs, and then your whole system just thermal throttles under load. I spent like three hours just routing everything perfectly behind the motherboard tray with these little velcro ties. 

So... yeah, I still like doing stuff like that. I wouldn't say I've lost interest completely.

It's so frustrating when these companies have closed ecosystems and you have to reverse-engineer their protocols just to turn on a light bulb. They actually look really good, they make the cable management inside the case so much cleaner. Cable management is actually super important, not just because it looks nice, but if you have this massive rat's nest of wires blocking the front intake fans, you're going to choke off the airflow to the motherboard VRMs, and then your whole system just thermal throttles under load. I don't think I've had whole days where I feel absolutely nothing. Like I said, I still get some enjoyment out of things like messing with my PC... 

Though, actually, speaking of my PC, I was looking at the system logs the other day because my event viewer was just throwing these random kernel-power errors. Error 41, which is usually a hardware issue, right? Like either the power supply is failing or there's a bad overclock. But I haven't even touched the BIOS settings since I built the thing. It's so weird. I started running MemTest86 off a bootable USB drive just to rule out the RAM, because sometimes bad memory can cause hard crashes that look like power issues. You have to let it run for like four passes, which takes hours, so I just left it running overnight. When I checked it in the morning, zero errors. So then I started thinking maybe it's the outlet itself, like dirty power from the wall. I live in this older apartment building and the wiring is probably from the 70s. I really should get a UPS—an uninterruptible power supply—that does active line conditioning. They're kind of expensive, but if you have a thousand-dollar graphics card, you don't want a random voltage spike from your neighbor turning on their vacuum cleaner to fry your motherboard. 

But, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, days where I don't care about anything. No, I don't think I've had days that are completely blank like that.

But yeah, that delayed connection feeling... it's kind of like that, but in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely lost interest in all activities, to the point where nothing gives you pleasure anymore, even things you used to really enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don't think I've completely lost interest in everything. I mean, I still like doing some things... I still get a little enjoyment out of messing with my PC setup. \n\nActually, I just tried to upgrade my graphics card last weekend. I finally managed to get my hands on a decent one at retail price, which is basically a miracle right now with all the scalpers and bots buying up the inventory in milliseconds. But when I went to install it, I realized my power supply didn't have the right PCIe cables. It's a fully modular power supply, but I must have thrown out the box with all the extra cables years ago when I moved. So I had to order these custom sleeved cables online. They actually look really good, they make the cable management inside the case so much cleaner. Cable management is actually super important, not just because it looks nice, but if you have this massive rat's nest of wires blocking the front intake fans, you're going to choke off the airflow to the motherboard VRMs, and then your whole system just thermal throttles under load. I spent like three hours just routing everything perfectly behind the motherboard tray with these little velcro ties. \n\nSo... yeah, I still like doing stuff like that. I wouldn't say I've lost interest completely.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced days where you felt absolutely no interest or pleasure in doing anything at all, even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don't think I've had whole days where I feel absolutely nothing. Like I said, I still get some enjoyment out of things like messing with my PC... \n\nThough, actually, speaking of my PC, I was looking at the system logs the other day because my event viewer was just throwing these random kernel-power errors. Error 41, which is usually a hardware issue, right? Like either the power supply is failing or there's a bad overclock. But I haven't even touched the BIOS settings since I built the thing. It's so weird. I started running MemTest86 off a bootable USB drive just to rule out the RAM, because sometimes bad memory can cause hard crashes that look like power issues. You have to let it run for like four passes, which takes hours, so I just left it running overnight. When I checked it in the morning, zero errors. So then I started thinking maybe it's the outlet itself, like dirty power from the wall. I live in this older apartment building and the wiring is probably from the 70s. I really should get a UPS—an uninterruptible power supply—that does active line conditioning. They're kind of expensive, but if you have a thousand-dollar graphics card, you don't want a random voltage spike from your neighbor turning on their vacuum cleaner to fry your motherboard. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, days where I don't care about anything. No, I don't think I've had days that are completely blank like that.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had periods of time lasting two weeks or longer where your mood was consistently low and you lost interest in most things?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 39 / computer_support_specialist / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressed_Mood\nmanifestation: \"Most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, like the things that used to reset me—putting on a record, cooking, tinkering with electronics—just don’t reach me anymore.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean, I guess it depends on what you mean by consistently low. Like... most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness. It's just sitting there. And the things that used to reset me, you know? Like putting on a record, cooking, or tinkering with my electronics—they just don't reach me anymore. 

Like, I used to spend hours just repairing old electronics. I bought this vintage Marantz receiver on eBay a while back—a 2270, one of the classic silver-face ones from the 70s. It was listed "for parts or repair," and the guy said the left channel was completely dead. Usually, when that happens on those old units, it's just a blown output transistor or maybe some bad capacitors on the amp board. I had this whole plan to recap the entire power supply and the amp section, swap out all those old electrolytic capacitors with nice new Nichicon audio-grade ones. You have to be super careful when you're desoldering on those old single-sided PCBs, though, because the copper pads will just lift right off the fiberglass if you apply too much heat. I bought a Hakko desoldering gun specifically for that project. It has this motorized vacuum pump that just sucks the solder right out of the through-hole, it's so much better than using that cheap copper braid that just burns your fingers. But the receiver is just sitting on my workbench right now, half disassembled. I look at it, and I know exactly what I need to do to fix it, but I just... I don't care. I don't have the energy to even plug in the soldering iron. 

So, yeah, it's been like that for... definitely more than two weeks. Just this heavy feeling where nothing really connects.

But yeah... everything just feels delayed now. It's just hard to tell sometimes, you know? \n\nLike, when I was in college, I used to pull these crazy all-nighters all the time. I'd stay up for 36 hours straight working on a single coding project. I remember this one time we had to build a basic operating system kernel from scratch for this advanced systems class. We were using C, mostly, and a little bit of Assembly language for the bootloader. Assembly is brutal because you're basically talking directly to the CPU registers. You have to manage the memory addresses manually, and if you mess up a single pointer, the whole system just crashes and throws a segmentation fault. We lived on those terrible energy drinks that tasted like battery acid and cheap pizza from this place down the street that stayed open until 4 AM. But the Bluetooth module on it just completely failed. I tried resetting it, unpairing it, clearing the Bluetooth cache on my phone... nothing. It's weird how dependent we get on those little microcontrollers. Like, I used to just step on a regular analog scale with the little dial that spins, but now if my scale can't send a data packet to a server halfway across the country, I feel like the weigh-in didn't even happen. I actually took the back off the scale to see if maybe a solder joint had come loose on the PCB, but it's all surface-mount components, so unless you have a hot air rework station and a microscope, you're pretty much out of luck. Anyway, no, I don't think my appetite has changed much.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: I don't have a plan to hurt myself or anything like that. But... I do catch myself thinking sometimes that it would just be easier if I didn't wake up, you know? Like, if I just faded out and nobody had to deal with me anymore. But I set up this whole smart home routine using Home Assistant—it's this open-source home automation platform that runs on a Raspberry Pi I have tucked behind my router. Anyway, I programmed it so that when my phone alarm goes off, it triggers a webhook that slowly fades up the smart bulbs in my bedroom over 15 minutes, to simulate a sunrise. It's supposed to be better for your circadian rhythm than just waking up in a dark room to a blaring noise. But last week, the API integration between Home Assistant and the smart bulb bridge just broke. No error logs, no timeout warnings, nothing. Just completely stopped talking to each other. I spent hours looking at the JSON payloads trying to figure out if the authentication token expired or if the manufacturer changed their API endpoints without telling anyone. It's so frustrating when these companies have closed ecosystems and you have to reverse-engineer their protocols just to turn on a light bulb. But yeah... sometimes I just think it would be easier not to wake up at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to concentrate on tasks, or felt like your thinking has slowed down significantly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have noticed that a bit. Like high latency.\n\nLatency is the worst, especially when you're trying to do remote support. They have terrible routing tables and the Wi-Fi antennas are practically useless if you're more than ten feet away or if there's a wall in the way. But... I do catch myself thinking sometimes that it would just be easier if I didn't wake up, you know? Like, if I just faded out and nobody had to deal with me anymore. It's just this passing thought that creeps in.\n\nSpeaking of waking up, I've been having the weirdest issue with my alarm clock lately. Well, it's not a clock, it's my phone, obviously. But I set up this whole smart home routine using Home Assistant—it's this open-source home automation platform that runs on a Raspberry Pi I have tucked behind my router. Anyway, I programmed it so that when my phone alarm goes off, it triggers a webhook that slowly fades up the smart bulbs in my bedroom over 15 minutes, to simulate a sunrise. It's supposed to be better for your circadian rhythm than just waking up in a dark room to a blaring noise. But last week, the API integration between Home Assistant and the smart bulb bridge just broke. No error logs, no timeout warnings, nothing. Just completely stopped talking to each other. I spent hours looking at the JSON payloads trying to figure out if the authentication token expired or if the manufacturer changed their API endpoints without telling anyone. It's so frustrating when these companies have closed ecosystems and you have to reverse-engineer their protocols just to turn on a light bulb. But yeah... sometimes I just think it would be easier not to wake up at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to concentrate on tasks, or felt like your thinking has slowed down significantly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have noticed that a bit. At work, I'll be looking at a simple support ticket, and my hands will just hover over the keyboard. It's like it takes me way longer to type out a basic response, almost like my brain and my body are running on a delayed connection, you know? Like high latency.\n\nLatency is the worst, especially when you're trying to do remote support. We use this screen-sharing software at work, and sometimes the connection is so bad that I'll click on a user's start menu and it takes like five seconds for it to actually open on my screen. It's infuriating. It usually happens when they're working from home on some terrible consumer-grade router that their ISP gave them. Those things are garbage. They have terrible routing tables and the Wi-Fi antennas are practically useless if you're more than ten feet away or if there's a wall in the way. I always tell people to just buy a decent mesh network system or at least a standalone router, but they never listen. They just complain that their internet is \"slow,\" when really it's just their local network that's congested because their kids are streaming 4K video and playing online games at the same time. You can't fix physics, you know? Bandwidth isn't infinite. But yeah, that delayed connection feeling... it's kind of like that, but in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely lost interest in all activities, to the point where nothing gives you pleasure anymore, even things you used to really enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don't think I've completely lost interest in everything. I mean, I still like doing some things... I still get a little enjoyment out of messing with my PC setup. \n\nActually, I just tried to upgrade my graphics card last weekend. I finally managed to get my hands on a decent one at retail price, which is basically a miracle right now with all the scalpers and bots buying up the inventory in milliseconds. But when I went to install it, I realized my power supply didn't have the right PCIe cables. It's a fully modular power supply, but I must have thrown out the box with all the extra cables years ago when I moved. So I had to order these custom sleeved cables online. They actually look really good, they make the cable management inside the case so much cleaner. Cable management is actually super important, not just because it looks nice, but if you have this massive rat's nest of wires blocking the front intake fans, you're going to choke off the airflow to the motherboard VRMs, and then your whole system just thermal throttles under load. I spent like three hours just routing everything perfectly behind the motherboard tray with these little velcro ties. \n\nSo... yeah, I still like doing stuff like that. I wouldn't say I've lost interest completely.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced days where you felt absolutely no interest or pleasure in doing anything at all, even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don't think I've had whole days where I feel absolutely nothing. Like I said, I still get some enjoyment out of things like messing with my PC... \n\nThough, actually, speaking of my PC, I was looking at the system logs the other day because my event viewer was just throwing these random kernel-power errors. Error 41, which is usually a hardware issue, right? Like either the power supply is failing or there's a bad overclock. But I haven't even touched the BIOS settings since I built the thing. It's so weird. I started running MemTest86 off a bootable USB drive just to rule out the RAM, because sometimes bad memory can cause hard crashes that look like power issues. You have to let it run for like four passes, which takes hours, so I just left it running overnight. When I checked it in the morning, zero errors. So then I started thinking maybe it's the outlet itself, like dirty power from the wall. I live in this older apartment building and the wiring is probably from the 70s. I really should get a UPS—an uninterruptible power supply—that does active line conditioning. They're kind of expensive, but if you have a thousand-dollar graphics card, you don't want a random voltage spike from your neighbor turning on their vacuum cleaner to fry your motherboard. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, days where I don't care about anything. No, I don't think I've had days that are completely blank like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of time lasting two weeks or longer where your mood was consistently low and you lost interest in most things?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I guess it depends on what you mean by consistently low. Like... most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness. It's just sitting there. And the things that used to reset me, you know? Like putting on a record, cooking, or tinkering with my electronics—they just don't reach me anymore. \n\nLike, I used to spend hours just repairing old electronics. I bought this vintage Marantz receiver on eBay a while back—a 2270, one of the classic silver-face ones from the 70s. It was listed \"for parts or repair,\" and the guy said the left channel was completely dead. Usually, when that happens on those old units, it's just a blown output transistor or maybe some bad capacitors on the amp board. I had this whole plan to recap the entire power supply and the amp section, swap out all those old electrolytic capacitors with nice new Nichicon audio-grade ones. You have to be super careful when you're desoldering on those old single-sided PCBs, though, because the copper pads will just lift right off the fiberglass if you apply too much heat. I bought a Hakko desoldering gun specifically for that project. It has this motorized vacuum pump that just sucks the solder right out of the through-hole, it's so much better than using that cheap copper braid that just burns your fingers. But the receiver is just sitting on my workbench right now, half disassembled. I look at it, and I know exactly what I need to do to fix it, but I just... I don't care. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, mornings are pretty rough. At night, I’ll just lie there for hours listening for apartment noises. The acoustics in this building are so weird. Like, I can hear this constant low-frequency hum from the building's HVAC system. 

I actually bought a calibrated measurement microphone—a UMIK-1—and hooked it up to Room EQ Wizard on my laptop just to map the frequency response of my bedroom. Turns out there's a massive resonance peak right around 60 hertz, which makes sense because that's the standard AC mains frequency in the US. It's probably an old ungrounded transformer vibrating against a joist somewhere in the ceiling. I tried putting up some mass-loaded vinyl to decouple the sound, but you really have to seal every single gap with acoustic sealant or the sound waves just diffract right around the barrier. It's exactly the same principle as applying thermal paste on a CPU cooler—if there's even a microscopic air pocket between the heat spreader and the block, the transfer efficiency just drops to zero and you get hot spots. I spent a whole weekend trying to isolate the wall, and it didn't even drop the decibel level by that much. 

But, um... what were you asking? Oh, right, my sleep. So yeah, I'll lie there listening to all that, and then I finally fall asleep, but I always wake up way before dawn. And when I do, I'm already tense and exhausted. It's like I never really powered down, you know? Just running on standby all night.

I just... I really can't shake this feeling that I've ruined things for everyone. I fell so far behind at work, and then letting the apartment situation get completely out of control... I just feel like a total failure. Like I deserve whatever bad thing happens to me next. \n\nThe apartment building is actually this really old brick walk-up, built in like 1924, I think. It has those ancient cast-iron radiators that clank so loud in the middle of the night. It sounds like someone is taking a metal wrench to the pipes, just banging on them at 3 AM. The super said it's because the boiler pressure is too high, which causes the steam to hit trapped condensation in the pipes. They call it a water hammer. You're actually supposed to put little wooden shims under the feet of the radiator to tilt it, just slightly, so the water drains back down to the boiler, but nobody ever does. I meant to fix mine, actually. I bought the shims from the hardware store and everything. But... yeah. I didn't do that either. I just feel like I mess everything up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been moving more slowly than usual, or have trouble sitting still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I've noticed that. It's really obvious at work. I'll just be sitting there, and my hands just hover over the keyboard. It takes me so much longer to answer even the simplest support tickets. It's almost like my brain and body are running on this delayed connection. \n\nSpeaking of connections, we had this massive server migration at work a few months ago, right before I started feeling really bad. We were switching from these old legacy physical servers to a fully cloud-based infrastructure. It was supposed to be seamless, but nothing ever is in IT. We had to map all these virtual LANs, and the IP routing tables were a complete mess because the previous guy who set them up didn't document anything. He used this really obscure naming convention for the subnets based on Lord of the Rings characters. Like, the main database was 'Rivendell' and the backup server was 'Helm's Deep.' It was funny for about five minutes until you actually had to troubleshoot a packet loss issue at 2 AM. I spent three whole weekends just untangling the DNS records. But yeah... everything just feels delayed now. Like I'm moving through molasses.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or having little energy most days, even when you haven't been doing much?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess... I mean, I'm not really sure if I'm more tired than usual. I don't think so. I haven't really noticed a huge difference in my energy, to be honest. It's just hard to tell sometimes, you know? \n\nLike, when I was in college, I used to pull these crazy all-nighters all the time. I'd stay up for 36 hours straight working on a single coding project. I remember this one time we had to build a basic operating system kernel from scratch for this advanced systems class. We were using C, mostly, and a little bit of Assembly language for the bootloader. Assembly is brutal because you're basically talking directly to the CPU registers. You have to manage the memory addresses manually, and if you mess up a single pointer, the whole system just crashes and throws a segmentation fault. We lived on those terrible energy drinks that tasted like battery acid and cheap pizza from this place down the street that stayed open until 4 AM. The guy who worked there, Tony, he always gave us extra garlic knots because he felt bad for us. We'd stumble out of the computer lab at dawn, completely exhausted, but it was this good kind of tired. A productive tired. Now, I just... I don't know. I guess I haven't really paid attention to my energy levels lately. I just kind of exist.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating much less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any big changes, to be honest. I mean, I guess I'm eating about the same as I usually do. I haven't been weighing myself or anything, so I couldn't tell you if I've lost weight. \n\nMy scale actually broke a few months ago. It was one of those smart scales that syncs via Bluetooth to an app on your phone, so it tracks your body fat percentage and bone mass and all that. But the Bluetooth module on it just completely failed. I tried resetting it, unpairing it, clearing the Bluetooth cache on my phone... nothing. It's weird how dependent we get on those little microcontrollers. Like, I used to just step on a regular analog scale with the little dial that spins, but now if my scale can't send a data packet to a server halfway across the country, I feel like the weigh-in didn't even happen. I actually took the back off the scale to see if maybe a solder joint had come loose on the PCB, but it's all surface-mount components, so unless you have a hot air rework station and a microscope, you're pretty much out of luck. Anyway, no, I don't think my appetite has changed much.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: I don't have a plan to hurt myself or anything like that. But... I do catch myself thinking sometimes that it would just be easier if I didn't wake up, you know? Like, if I just faded out and nobody had to deal with me anymore. It's just this passing thought that creeps in.\n\nSpeaking of waking up, I've been having the weirdest issue with my alarm clock lately. Well, it's not a clock, it's my phone, obviously. But I set up this whole smart home routine using Home Assistant—it's this open-source home automation platform that runs on a Raspberry Pi I have tucked behind my router. Anyway, I programmed it so that when my phone alarm goes off, it triggers a webhook that slowly fades up the smart bulbs in my bedroom over 15 minutes, to simulate a sunrise. It's supposed to be better for your circadian rhythm than just waking up in a dark room to a blaring noise. But last week, the API integration between Home Assistant and the smart bulb bridge just broke. No error logs, no timeout warnings, nothing. Just completely stopped talking to each other. I spent hours looking at the JSON payloads trying to figure out if the authentication token expired or if the manufacturer changed their API endpoints without telling anyone. It's so frustrating when these companies have closed ecosystems and you have to reverse-engineer their protocols just to turn on a light bulb. But yeah... sometimes I just think it would be easier not to wake up at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to concentrate on tasks, or felt like your thinking has slowed down significantly?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have noticed that a bit. At work, I'll be looking at a simple support ticket, and my hands will just hover over the keyboard. It's like it takes me way longer to type out a basic response, almost like my brain and my body are running on a delayed connection, you know? Like high latency.\n\nLatency is the worst, especially when you're trying to do remote support. We use this screen-sharing software at work, and sometimes the connection is so bad that I'll click on a user's start menu and it takes like five seconds for it to actually open on my screen. It's infuriating. It usually happens when they're working from home on some terrible consumer-grade router that their ISP gave them. Those things are garbage. They have terrible routing tables and the Wi-Fi antennas are practically useless if you're more than ten feet away or if there's a wall in the way. I always tell people to just buy a decent mesh network system or at least a standalone router, but they never listen. They just complain that their internet is \"slow,\" when really it's just their local network that's congested because their kids are streaming 4K video and playing online games at the same time. You can't fix physics, you know? Bandwidth isn't infinite. But yeah, that delayed connection feeling... it's kind of like that, but in my head.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely lost interest in all activities, to the point where nothing gives you pleasure anymore, even things you used to really enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don't think I've completely lost interest in everything. I mean, I still like doing some things... I still get a little enjoyment out of messing with my PC setup. \n\nActually, I just tried to upgrade my graphics card last weekend. I finally managed to get my hands on a decent one at retail price, which is basically a miracle right now with all the scalpers and bots buying up the inventory in milliseconds. But when I went to install it, I realized my power supply didn't have the right PCIe cables. It's a fully modular power supply, but I must have thrown out the box with all the extra cables years ago when I moved. So I had to order these custom sleeved cables online. They actually look really good, they make the cable management inside the case so much cleaner. Cable management is actually super important, not just because it looks nice, but if you have this massive rat's nest of wires blocking the front intake fans, you're going to choke off the airflow to the motherboard VRMs, and then your whole system just thermal throttles under load. I spent like three hours just routing everything perfectly behind the motherboard tray with these little velcro ties. \n\nSo... yeah, I still like doing stuff like that. I wouldn't say I've lost interest completely.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced days where you felt absolutely no interest or pleasure in doing anything at all, even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don't think I've had whole days where I feel absolutely nothing. Like I said, I still get some enjoyment out of things like messing with my PC... \n\nThough, actually, speaking of my PC, I was looking at the system logs the other day because my event viewer was just throwing these random kernel-power errors. Error 41, which is usually a hardware issue, right? Like either the power supply is failing or there's a bad overclock. But I haven't even touched the BIOS settings since I built the thing. It's so weird. I started running MemTest86 off a bootable USB drive just to rule out the RAM, because sometimes bad memory can cause hard crashes that look like power issues. You have to let it run for like four passes, which takes hours, so I just left it running overnight. When I checked it in the morning, zero errors. So then I started thinking maybe it's the outlet itself, like dirty power from the wall. I live in this older apartment building and the wiring is probably from the 70s. I really should get a UPS—an uninterruptible power supply—that does active line conditioning. They're kind of expensive, but if you have a thousand-dollar graphics card, you don't want a random voltage spike from your neighbor turning on their vacuum cleaner to fry your motherboard. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, days where I don't care about anything. No, I don't think I've had days that are completely blank like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of time lasting two weeks or longer where your mood was consistently low and you lost interest in most things?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I guess it depends on what you mean by consistently low. Like... most days I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness. It's just sitting there. And the things that used to reset me, you know? Like putting on a record, cooking, or tinkering with my electronics—they just don't reach me anymore. \n\nLike, I used to spend hours just repairing old electronics. I bought this vintage Marantz receiver on eBay a while back—a 2270, one of the classic silver-face ones from the 70s. It was listed \"for parts or repair,\" and the guy said the left channel was completely dead. Usually, when that happens on those old units, it's just a blown output transistor or maybe some bad capacitors on the amp board. I had this whole plan to recap the entire power supply and the amp section, swap out all those old electrolytic capacitors with nice new Nichicon audio-grade ones. You have to be super careful when you're desoldering on those old single-sided PCBs, though, because the copper pads will just lift right off the fiberglass if you apply too much heat. I bought a Hakko desoldering gun specifically for that project. It has this motorized vacuum pump that just sucks the solder right out of the through-hole, it's so much better than using that cheap copper braid that just burns your fingers. But the receiver is just sitting on my workbench right now, half disassembled. I look at it, and I know exactly what I need to do to fix it, but I just... I don't care. I don't have the energy to even plug in the soldering iron. \n\nSo, yeah, it's been like that for... definitely more than two weeks. Just this heavy feeling where nothing really connects.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced early morning waking—like waking up several hours before you intended to—or have you noticed that your mood is particularly worse in the morning?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, mornings are pretty rough. At night, I’ll just lie there for hours listening for apartment noises. The acoustics in this building are so weird. Like, I can hear this constant low-frequency hum from the building's HVAC system. \n\nI actually bought a calibrated measurement microphone—a UMIK-1—and hooked it up to Room EQ Wizard on my laptop just to map the frequency response of my bedroom. Turns out there's a massive resonance peak right around 60 hertz, which makes sense because that's the standard AC mains frequency in the US. It's probably an old ungrounded transformer vibrating against a joist somewhere in the ceiling. I tried putting up some mass-loaded vinyl to decouple the sound, but you really have to seal every single gap with acoustic sealant or the sound waves just diffract right around the barrier. It's exactly the same principle as applying thermal paste on a CPU cooler—if there's even a microscopic air pocket between the heat spreader and the block, the transfer efficiency just drops to zero and you get hot spots. I spent a whole weekend trying to isolate the wall, and it didn't even drop the decibel level by that much. \n\nBut, um... what were you asking? Oh, right, my sleep. So yeah, I'll lie there listening to all that, and then I finally fall asleep, but I always wake up way before dawn. And when I do, I'm already tense and exhausted. It's like I never really powered down, you know? Just running on standby all night.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Turns out there's a massive resonance peak right around 60 hertz, which makes sense because that's the standard AC mains frequency in the US. It's probably an old ungrounded transformer vibrating against a joist somewhere in the ceiling. I tried putting up some mass-loaded vinyl to decouple the sound, but you really have to seal every single gap with acoustic sealant or the sound waves just diffract right around the barrier. It's exactly the same principle as applying thermal paste on a CPU cooler—if there's even a microscopic air pocket between the heat spreader and the block, the transfer efficiency just drops to zero and you get hot spots. I spent a whole weekend trying to isolate the wall, and it didn't even drop the decibel level by that much. \n\nBut, um... what were you asking? Oh, right, my sleep. So yeah, I'll lie there listening to all that, and then I finally fall asleep, but I always wake up way before dawn. And when I do, I'm already tense and exhausted. It's like I never really powered down, you know? Just running on standby all night."
